NEW_CANDIDATE VARIABLE_STAR_SHAPE HARMONIC_OF_SIGNAL_1 VERY_DEEP_EB_LIKELY TOO_BIG_FOR_PLANET

Failed the automatic checks. Here's why:

  • Variable star. Between the dips the brightness doesn't sit flat. The star itself is changing (spots rotating into view, pulsations, or a stretched-out binary), and the search latched on to those wiggles.
  • Echo. Its period is a simple multiple or fraction of signal 1 on the same star, so it's most likely that same signal showing up again.
  • Way too deep. The star loses 18.7% of its light. A Jupiter in front of a Sun-like star blocks about 1%. Dips this deep almost always mean a second star.
  • Too big. With a host 3.5× the Sun's size, a dip this deep needs an object about 167 Earth radii across. Jupiter is about 11, and the biggest planets known top out near 22. Something that big is a star.

  • Variable star. Between the dips the brightness doesn't sit flat. The star itself is changing (spots rotating into view, pulsations, or a stretched-out binary), and the search latched on to those wiggles.
  • Only 2 dips in the data. You need at least three evenly spaced dips before a period means anything.

  • One dip does all the work. A single event carries 77% of the signal, so it could be a one-off glitch or a stray bump in the data.
  • Impossible orbit. Anything circling every 14.41 days would sit about 31.4 solar radii out, which is inside a 112.6-solar-radius star. The dip probably comes from a smaller star blended into the same pixels, or the catalogue size is off.

  • Impossible orbit. Anything circling every 1.13 days would sit about 5.8 solar radii out, which is inside a 8.8-solar-radius star. The dip probably comes from a smaller star blended into the same pixels, or the catalogue size is off.

  • Alternating dips don't match. Odd-numbered dips average 4684 ppm and even ones 8044 ppm (23σ apart). A planet makes the same dip every time. Two different stars taking turns eclipsing each other, at twice this period, gives alternating depths.
  • Too big. With a host 7.8× the Sun's size, a dip this deep needs an object about 68 Earth radii across. Jupiter is about 11, and the biggest planets known top out near 22. Something that big is a star.
  • Impossible orbit. Anything circling every 0.65 days would sit about 4.0 solar radii out, which is inside a 7.8-solar-radius star. The dip probably comes from a smaller star blended into the same pixels, or the catalogue size is off.

NEW_CANDIDATE VARIABLE_STAR_SHAPE ODD_EVEN_MISMATCH

Failed the automatic checks. Here's why:

  • Variable star. Between the dips the brightness doesn't sit flat. The star itself is changing (spots rotating into view, pulsations, or a stretched-out binary), and the search latched on to those wiggles.
  • Alternating dips don't match. Odd-numbered dips average 442 ppm and even ones 763 ppm (7σ apart). A planet makes the same dip every time. Two different stars taking turns eclipsing each other, at twice this period, gives alternating depths.

NEW_CANDIDATE ONLY_2_TRANSITS TOO_BIG_FOR_PLANET ONE_EVENT_DOMINATES

Failed the automatic checks. Here's why:

  • Only 2 dips in the data. You need at least three evenly spaced dips before a period means anything.
  • Too big. With a host 74.2× the Sun's size, a dip this deep needs an object about 265 Earth radii across. Jupiter is about 11, and the biggest planets known top out near 22. Something that big is a star.
  • One dip does all the work. A single event carries 67% of the signal, so it could be a one-off glitch or a stray bump in the data.
  • Impossible orbit. Anything circling every 19.24 days would sit about 38.1 solar radii out, which is inside a 74.2-solar-radius star. The dip probably comes from a smaller star blended into the same pixels, or the catalogue size is off.

NEW_CANDIDATE VARIABLE_STAR_SHAPE HARMONIC_OF_SIGNAL_1 HARMONIC_OF_SIGNAL_2 ODD_EVEN_MISMATCH SECONDARY_ECLIPSE NOT_A_DIP

Failed the automatic checks. Here's why:

  • Variable star. Between the dips the brightness doesn't sit flat. The star itself is changing (spots rotating into view, pulsations, or a stretched-out binary), and the search latched on to those wiggles.
  • Echo. Its period is a simple multiple or fraction of signal 1 on the same star, so it's most likely that same signal showing up again.
  • Echo. Its period is a simple multiple or fraction of signal 2 on the same star, so it's most likely that same signal showing up again.
  • Alternating dips don't match. Odd-numbered dips average 98 ppm and even ones -274 ppm (41σ apart). A planet makes the same dip every time. Two different stars taking turns eclipsing each other, at twice this period, gives alternating depths.
  • Second dip at the halfway point (89 ppm, 18σ). A planet is almost invisible when it passes behind its star. Two stars eclipsing each other usually show a dip both ways.
  • Not a dip at all. At this period the star gets brighter, not dimmer. Planets can't do that.

Open diagnostic plot

No signals match that filter.

Ground rules. Nothing on this site is a confirmed planet unless it says known planet, meaning astronomers confirmed it long before Kepler showed up. A candidate is only a candidate. The pipeline doesn't do pixel-level vetting, and most signals like these turn out to be eclipsing binaries, blended neighbour stars, or instrument noise. Art marked artist's impression is generated, not observed.

Data: NASA TESS via MAST, NASA Exoplanet Archive, ExoFOP. Search: planet-hunter (BLS/TLS + automatic vetting). RSS
